## On Leaves and Flowers (Fragment) Frag. on Leaves & Flowers edited

JEFrag. on Leaves & Flowers. ANTS. See "Notes on Natural Science," no. 48, in WJE 6:242-46, esp. p. 245, n. 6, for parallel passage, which JE deleted in the original.

Remember this

Leaves fruits & flower & fruit not branches but the substance of the trunks to shew

that there is a threefold substance in the trunk, the leaves the Extremity of the first the

flowers of the 2’d the fruit of the third and the seeds only a Continuation of

the branches of the tree

to Prove About fruit, tis Only the substance of the trunk swoln by the seed how I

have seen a Green twig swoln Just like an apple Only by the space of [ ]

## On Marital Duties (Fragment) Marital Duties (frag.)

Jonathan EdwardsOn Marital Duties. Fragment. BRBL, in Sermon on Ps. 116:162, Nov. 1749, LL. 1v.-2r. Possibly a response to a question posed in a meeting of the Hampshire Ministerial Association, or at an ecclesiastical council. Hand and ink appear to be late 1730s or early 1740s. Shorn at top, and incomplete at end.

whereby the [. . .] pose that by putting away [ ] [ ] [. . .] motion in him a bodily action in him [lately][^002-note1] here & putting her forth & shutting [doors] against her or the like . If he will not suffer her to so live with him if it appears & is evidently if it evident if it be manifest whether by words that he speaks or by Long experience of his practice that he will not suffer her to live with him or which is the same thing by his voluntary behaviour renders it intolerable & useless for her to live with him he in effect puts her away & she in departing from him is to Looked upon no other wise than passive in the affair . If he in words should threaten her & declare that if she did live with him that he would Kill either by poisoning or starving her or any other way . that she was not passive & she should dep on this should depart from would i should we not say that he had put her away & that was Justifiable in departi not passive in departing. And whether the danger that he will thus destroy her appears by what he says in words or what is from rationally Judged from Long experience of his practice voluntary practice how does that alter the Case . it is supposed in the Question that this is what she is indeed threaten'd with or what is Likely to come upon her. : He that is said by experience to have Cast off the fundamental duties of marrige towards his wife may Justly be Looked upon as having put away his wife . such Fundamental duties of marrige I suppose they will be allowed to be that are not only necessary to the speciall more special designs & purposes of marriage but also to the higher purposes of Life & subsistence in the Θ which the Pur to which the more special Purposes of marriage must be supposed Inferiour & subordinate on which all other purposes of this Life of ma whether of marriage or any thing else depend de depend as the so far as they concern that person do depend. If such duties are cast off by an husband the wife is not only not scandalous for departing but tis her duty to depart the Great Law of self Preservation obliges her to it. Tis not Gods manner to Establish one Command on the

[. . .]

2. Edited version:

whereby the [. . .]pose that by [. . .] motion, a bodily action in him [lately][^002-note2] here, and putting her forth, and shutting [doors] against her or the like. If it be manifest, whether by words that he speaks or by long experience of his practice, that he will not suffer her to live with him, or, which is the same thing, by his voluntary behavior renders it intolerable and useless for her to live with him, he in effect puts her away, and she in departing from him is to [be] looked upon no otherwise than passive in the affair. If he in words should threaten her and declare that if she did live with him that he would kill [her], either by poisoning or starving her or any other way, and she on this should depart from [him], should we not say that he had put her away and that [she] was not passive in departing? And whether the danger that he will thus destroy her appears by what he says in words or what is rationally judged from long experience of his voluntary practice, how does that alter the case? It is supposed in the Question that this is what she is indeed threatened with, or what is likely to come upon her, and practice and experience are more certain notifications of anything than words: he that is said by experience to have cast off the fundamental duties of marriage towards his wife, may justly be looked upon as having put away his wife. Such fundamental duties of marriage I suppose they will be allowed to be that are not only necessary to the more special designs and purposes of marriage, but also to the higher purposes of life and subsistence in the world, on which all other purposes of this life, whether of marriage or anything else, so far as they concern that person, do depend. If such duties are cast off by an husband, the wife is not only not scandalous for departing, but ‘tis her duty to depart; the great law of self-preservation obliges her to it. ‘Tis not God's manner to establish one command on the [. . .]

[^002-note1]: The two bracketed words on this line contain conjectural readings, the tops of the words being shorn off.

[^002-note2]: The two bracketed words in this sentence contain conjectural readings, the tops of the words being shorn off in the MS.

## Account Book Account Book

Jonathan EdwardsJuly 1733 Lent to mr Billing of sunderland the Church History of Geneva

Aug 1733 Lent to mr Allis the second vol of Millars History of the Propagation of Xtianity

Mr Allis also has in his hands mr Foxcrofts sermon Preparatory to the Choice of a minister

Lent to John Miller Mr Stoddards Cases of Conscience Respecting the Countrey

Married Lieut Jospeh King & Mindwell Porter Aug 30 1733.

Lent to Dan Webs wife sep 1733. book published by Nathaniel stone.

Lent to Mr Hopkins 2d vol of Prideaux sep. 27. 1733.

Lent to Elisha Marsh of Hadley Octob. 4 1733 D[ ]ds Rhetorick

sent a napkin to Hatfield Octob. 1733 to cover some of [ ] Books.

A Description of a Couple of steer Calves belonging to me Octob. 1733 The biggest mostly Red But whitest under his Belly his taily mostly white but something speckled with Brown & he has [a] spot of white on his Backbone near his Tail some white on the Inside of Each hind leg of his front legs and a small speck of white on [ ] of each of his hind Legs & two little specks [streaks?] of white on his Back bone near his shoulders

The other [Last] Chiefly of a Darker Colour & Brindle Whitish under his Belly some white on Each Edge of his Tail. White specks on the Insides of Each hind Leg

An heifer about 3 years old of a Brown or dark red, white over most of her face some white under her chin some Little white under her Body, Just behind her fore legs.

[^003-note1] A steer two year old Red but white fac'd white under his belly a spot of white on his Back Bone near [on] his tail & a small Lock of white Hairs between his shoulders & a spot of white on the Inside of Both his hin'd Legs & a streak of white on the Inside of his Tail

An Heifer two year old of a Dark Brown and white on her forehead & a speck of white on the Right side of her nose

Lent to Mr Rawson one of Mr Watts's Catechisms Novem 1733

Novem 15 1733. Married John summers & Elizabeth Alexander & Calen Pumroy & Thankfull Phelps.

Novem 22. 1733. Married Jospeh Allen & Elizabeth Parsons

Decem 26 1733. Married Samll Lancton & sarah Wait

David Web[ster] Came to our House Jan 11. 1733/4

Married Benjn Alvord & Ruth Alexander Jan Jan 17. 1733.

sent down Millars History to my Father Feb. 1733/4

Married supply Kingsley & Elizabeth strong February 14. 1733/4

Lent to Jth Rust Mr. Williams's Discourse on Infant Baptism. Feb. 1733/4.

[^003-note2] Directions how to find the Cranberry Place at Hog Island

After I Get over Wiltons meadow brook and Come to the ascent of the Hill about 20 or thirty Rod beyond the Brook to take the Left hand Path to Go up the Hill and about 10 or 15 Rod beyond the ascent of the Hill there I have mark'd a Pine Tree at the Left hand near a Rod from the Path by Beating off the Bark with a stone to the wood a Round spot Like a mark to shoot at and Laid several stones together at the Root of the Tree. in a Line from that Tree to the Bottom of the Descent from the Peak of mount Tom, is a Tall spruce Tree It may be about 40 Rod out of the Path the spot of Cranberries is about 7 Rod Northeast from that spruce Tree Just under a small White oak Tree the Top of which seems to be dead & broken off which stands on the West side of it & just by it may be two Rod off North west Lies a dry Log the stump being toward the northeast, that End that Lies toward the s. w. Lying up from the Ground

[4] Receiv'd of my salary of Gideon Lyman Constable March. 1734. 3ll-0-0

Recieved of Daniel King Constable March 15 1734. 35ll-0-0 of my salary

Recieved of Daniel King April 15 1734. 15 Pound in 3 Pound Bill of Road Island Last Emission. Which afterw he Changed & gave so much in merchts notes

Recievd of Gideon Lyman Constable 10ll of my salary on Aprill 22. 1734

Married sam. Marshal & Deborah Dudley April 25 1734.

Married Noah sheldon & Mary Bascom May 1 1734.

Married Jospeh Clark & Mary Wright May 2. 1734.

May 11 1734 Reciev'd of Gideon Lyman Constable that which together with his other Payments made it up 50ll

Recieved of Gideon Lyman May 20. 10ll

Married Ezekiel Bascom of Deerfield & Rebekah Clary of Northampton on May 23. 1734.

Delivered to Deac. Allen to be Lay'd out at Road Island May 23 1734 9ll-5-0. ----- & 30sh afterwards to buy a Cheese with

Recievd of Dan King Latter End of May 10. Pound of salary

Lent 2 vol of Ladies Library to Lyddia Dwight. June 1734

July 25. 1734. Married Jonathan Clark & Thankfull Edwards

septem. 1734. Lent to Jonathan strong the Religious Courtship

[5] septem 5 --- Gideon Lyman made it up 70ll-10-0. of my salary so that there remaind 8s. due to me

septem 6. Lent to Judah Hutchinson Junr Clarks Evidence of Natural & Reveal'd Religion.

David Webster went Down the Countrey to the Election on Wednesday & Returned on a saturday the week following.

David Webster went from our House July 8. 1734.

Reciev'd of Daniel King Constable. 27ll. 10s-0 sep. 25. 1734

Recievd of Dan. King Constable novem. 2. 2ll-0-0

Married samuel Hindell & Rebecca Leonard Novem 13. 1734.

Recieved of Daniel King Constable 11ll Decem 5. 1734

Lent to Cousin Bec [Rebecca?] Micks Mr Watts's sermons & Last volume of Ladies Library.

Married Gideon Hall & Thankfull Alexander & Nathaniel Dwight & Hannah Lyman. Jan 2. 1734/5

Married Jonathan Clap & submit strong Jan 23.

Recievd of Daniel King Feb. 14. 5ll 10s

Reciev'd of Noah Clark Constable 30ll-1-0. May 9.th 1735

[^003-note4] Recieved of Noah Wright Constable Aug 29That which together with former 18s That which with former Payments made it up 80ll-0s-0.

Lent to married Ephraim Danks & Abigail Web

Disposed of four of Mr. Marshfields Books Feb. 1736 Jth strong & Jth Rust Each of them owes for one of them.

Let Mr. Williams of Hatfield have 1/2 a Doz. of those Books. & mr Ashley two & Ebenezer Pumroy 12. who all said they would be accoutable for them to Mr Marshfield

Recieved of John Miller Constable 25 Pounds two shillings of my salary March 19. 1736.

[^003-note5] June 1736 Lent the first vol. of the Guardian to Mrs. sarah Parsons.

sent 5 Pound to Boston by Capt Dwight & 5 Pounds more by Mr Patrick Novem 8 1736

Novem 23. 1736 married Waitstill strong & Esther Root.

Novem 30 1736 married stephen sheldon & Thankfull King.

Another soldier went from our House out to the New town on Tuesday after Dinner, the Last Day of septem. & returned next Day before Dinner a Lecture Day.

Two of the soldiers went to Deerfield & were absent two meals

delivered to Mr Bellamy 30 Pound to buy sheep Octob. 21. 1746. & Lent to him Manton on James & the 2 Vol. of Ladies Library & one Vol. of Family Instructor

Lent Mr. searl my Discourse on that Question What is the Power of Ecclesiastical Councils Latter End of Octob. 1746.

Lent Dr. Wigglesworth on original sin to Job strong. Latter End of Octob 1746.

The soldiers went from our House Novemb. 3. 1746. Two others came the same Day at night & went away the next Day but one after Dinner & that Evening came, Peter Downer.

He went away on saturday Novem 8. & Came again the next Wednesday before Dinner.

Lent Dr Wigglesworth on original sin to Mrs [Mr.?] searl.

Lent to Preserved Bartlet Dr. Colman on satans Fiery Darts.

[^003-note11] sent to my Father by Mr Jth Hunt Mr Princes sermon on the suppression of the Rebellion & Mr Whitefields & Mr Williamss Answer to Mr Croswell & Mr Willisons sermons against Piper [Popes?] & Britains Remembrancer.

sent to Mr Bellamy in April 1747 in a letter to Moses Lyman April. 1747. Returned to Mr Bellamy at Windsor the money I had of him viz 7 Pounds to buy Turretine

Col. stoddard told me the latter End of septem. 1747. that money when he was last in Boston which was last June that money was 52s. an ounce & that Maj. Williams had since told Him that it was 3L an ounce

Note all the Bills I sent to Boston to Mrs sarah Green were Bills Received of my Father but one 40sill. Bill new Tenour or Eight Pound old Tenour that had Jonathan Clap. written on the Backside at top. with such a Cross [X] above his name & a 20s Bill Road Island New Tenour & 4L old Tenour whereon is written on the backside samll Handly The summ that was sent By Benj. Alvord to Mrs Green at Boston. was 130L old Tenour

Decem. 3. Married Aaron Baker & Jemima Clark.

Received of Benjamin Alvord Constable 3L of my salary paid for me in Boston for Indigo . March 1748.

The Bargain made with Caleb Pomroy Respecting my Land at Pomroys Meadow April 4. 1748 for this year was that he was to bring me half the Flax in the sheaf rotted [rosted?] & a third of the Produce of the Rest of the Land and leave the Land well fenced according to the former Bargain

Comes to 42L-10s-0- sterl. so that there remains of my salary 27L-10s-0d sterling

Capt Clap

Ens. Parsons[?]

Jos Wright. June 1734

Deac Allen . Jth strong . Moses Lyman. Aug. 1734

A.D. 1736. May the 5 was the first afternoon I did without fire

May 11 was the first day that any blossoms appeared on our cherry Trees & there were observable no blossoms on the Peach Trees tho they in other years used to blossom first.

May 12. had fire only Early in the morning & it was a very warm Day so that I kept a window open Great Part of the Day . & from this Time Left off Keeping fires

May 14 the blossoms began to appear on some of our apple Trees & Plumb trees. & It Grew warmer & warmer from this Time & in a very little Time after this became steadily very Hot weather

May A. D. 1737. May 7. was the first Day that blossoms began to appear on the Cherry trees before my House.

A. D. 1741. May 9. was the

A. D. 1747. April 2. The little white flowers a considerable number of them were ente blown in my Pastures. had a Mess of Herbs the same Day.

Jonathan EdwardsAccount Book leaf, Feb.-Oct. 1753, Huntington Library, San Marino, Cal. Tipped into copy of J.E. Woodbridge, *The Memorial Volume of the Edwards Family Meeting at Stockbridge, Mass.* (Boston, 1871). Pp. 69-70 of Account Book (BRBL). Some letters are obscured in the gutter.

To 3 small ones with Psalms . 3-18-

what it was agreed Hannis[?] should have for Interpreting was 2s-4d L.[?]

vol of Perkins & simile's & sentences

[^005-note1]: **marginal.** [r.]

[^005-note2]: Abner Ly[man] (1701-1774).

[^005-note3]: Benjamin Alvord, Jr. (1695-1772), a Northampton constable.

[^005-note4]: **marginal.** [v.]

[^005-note5]: Most likely Robert Abercrombie, pastor of Pelham, Mass.

[^005-note6]: Solomon Stoddard, *An Answer to Some Cases of Conscience Respecting the Country* (Boston, 1722).

[^005-note7]: Born 1722, the daughter of Joseph Wright, Jr. and Ruth Hannum Wright.

[^005-note8]: Elnathan Wright (1716-1801) and Mary Hannum (b. 1720-1794).

[^005-note9]: Ebenezer Bartlett, Jr. (b. 1721) and Rachel King (b. 1720).

[^005-note10]: Rev. Jonathan Judd, minister of Southampton; Richard Montagu, *The acts and monuments of the Church before Christ incarnate* (London, 1642).

[^005-note11]: Ebenezer Wait (1710-1753) and Abigail Janes (b. 1721; m. Samuel Judd, 1775).

[^005-note12]: Rev. Samuel Hopkins of Housatonnuck or Sheffield; "Body of Divinity" could be Samuel Willard, *Complete Body of Divinity* (1726), or Peter van Mastricht, *Theoretico-Practica Theologia* (1715), or Thomas Ridgley, *A body of divinity: wherein the doctrines of the Christian religion are explained and defended: being the substance of several lectures on the Assembly's Larger catechism / by Thomas Ridgley* (1731-1733).

[^005-note13]: Ministerial candidate Amos Munson (1719-1748) of Washington, Conn.; JE, "Discourse on that Question What is the Power of Ecclesiastical Councils," an MS no longer extant; JE also lent it to John Searl in Oct. 1746 (Account Book, BRBL, p. 33).

[^005-note14]: Possibly a notation recording the marriage of Noah Clark, Jr. (1719-1789) and Rachel Phelps (b. 1745), who moved to Granby; he became a Baptist.

[^006-note1]: **marginal.** 20

[^006-note2]: Possibly hatmaker Ebenezer Hunt (1703-88).

[^006-note3]: Probably John Hulbert (1676-1737); which edition of Isaac Watts's *The First catechism of the principles of religion, or, the catechism for a young child, to be begun at three or four years old* he borrowed cannot be ascertained. By 1734, the work was in its fifth edition in London.

[^006-note4]: JE's brother-in-law, Rev. Samuel Hopkins of West Springfield; Moses Lyman (1713-68) settled in Goshen, Conn., in 1739 and became a deacon, magistrate, and representative to the General Court

[^006-note5]: Possibly John Hannam, Jr. (1676-1757) of Southampton.

[^006-note6]: Probably John Hunt (1712-85), a militia officer who reached the rank of captain.

[^006-note7]: Rev. Samuel Hopkins, JE's disciple.

[^006-note8]: Josiah Sheldon (1695-1757) had two daughters, Anne (b. 1732, m. Aaron Cook of Hadley) and, more likely for this entry, Ruth (b. 1736, d. unm. in Litchfield, c. 1806).

## Deposition on Warham Mather Warham Mather Case

Jonathan Edwards

### Testimony on Warham Mather, 1749

Warham Mather, born in 1666, was the son of Northampton's first minister, Eleazar Mather. After some early attempts at preaching and teaching, he served as a justice in New Haven and died there in 1745. His will was presented but not approved because there were witnesses who testified regarding the "unsoundness of his mind," [^011-note1] or in modern terms, dementia. In January 1748, the court divided his estate equally between nine immediate and distant relatives, the distant ones being children by his mother's second marriage to Rev. Solomon Stoddard. The immediate or blood relatives appealed the decision, and in March 1749 the court changed its decision in favor of the Mathers.

In September 1749, Edwards was called upon to submit a testimony about Mather's mental health, which was attested by Justice Timothy Dwight. The stage of the case to which his deposition related is uncertain. Perhaps the Stoddard relations re-appealed and asked their cousin for help. For his part, Edwards recalled having a conversation with Mather in April 1745, just a few months before his death, that convinced him that Mather's memory was severely affected.

*****

The manuscript, a single quarto leaf, is in the collections of the New Haven Colony Historical Society. It is countersigned on the verso at the top, "Opened In Windham Superiour Court / Sept: Term 1749 / pr Wm. Pitkin Assist," and, in another hand, written perpendicularly to the above notation along the margin, "Jonth Edwards." The deposition is in Edwards' hand, and the affidavit below it is written and signed by Timothy Dwight.

The Testimony of Jonathan Edwards of Northampton Clerk.

I Testify & declare that I was at Newhaven, at the House of Mr. Warham Mather, in one of the latter years of his Life, while he lived at his own House, and was in discourse with Him concerning his Native Town & Mr. Stoddard's Family; and it appeared very manifest to me by his discourse that He had very remarkably forgotten some of those Things relating to the foresaid Town & Family, which I supposed must needs be most known & obvious to Him when of sound memory. & particularly, according to the best of my Remembrance, He enquired whether Col. Stoddard lived at Northampton; and manifested his having wholly forgotten that there was any little River that ran through Northampton, altho' Northampton was his native Town, and his Fathers House was near the little River, & his Fathers Home-Lot bounded upon it. He ak also asked me who my mother was; Tho' He had been many years particularly acquainted with me, almost from my Childhood; I having lived at New-Haven, in all, about six years . in all which Time I was very Conversant at his House, by Reason of the Relation; and for several months boarded at his House. I am satisfied these Things passed four years ago last April.

Jonathan Edwards [^011-note2]

Hampshire Northampton Sept.r 2d 1749 The Revnd Mr Jona Edwards Subscriber to the Above Declaration personally appeared in Northampton Aforesd before me the subscriber one of His Majestes Justices of the Peace for sd County & made Solemn Oath to the Truth of the Same Declaration

Timothy Dwight

[^011-note3]

## John Sargeant Valedictory, 1729 (Excerpt) Sargeant Valedictory 1729

John Sargenat*A Valedictorian Oration by John Sergeant, Delivered at Yale College in the Year 1729* (New York: Henry W. Turner, 1882), excerpted. The original ms. is at Williams College, Williamstown, Mass.

[After opening remarks and compliments to Rector Williams and Tutors Noyes and Rout, Sargeant turns to JE:]

'Tis a renual of our late sad grief not yet forgotten [^012-note1] to address ourselves to MR. EDWARDS. Your dearest presence, most gentle sir, afresh renews the saddest grief your late leaving us so deep impressed on our minds. When we see the same mildness, the same ingenuous goodness visible in your countenance, while we behold it, expressed by the same legible characters whereby we used to read the inward happy mind, how can we but find the same grateful, tender affections moving us toward you by which you was wont to move us with easy pleasure in our diligent acquisitions of truth. As everything mean, sordid and unmanly was at the farthest distance removed from yourself, so (we are witnesses) in governing and instructing us you was wholly a stranger to all unmanly, sordid or mean arguments by which to persuade us to the study of philosophy or practice of virtue. As the things themselves had in them eternal and immutable reasons why they should be studied and practiced, so fully acquainted with their intrinsick worth, you could display to us the attractive beauty captivative [^012-note2] of any intelligent, reasonable mind, not void of all the seed of goodness. What refined pleasure did we not enjoy when you used to lead us unerring to the darkest mysteries of language, art and science? You could teach us as you had done to reject each erroneous, prejudiced opinion (the hatefull fetters that chain up the mind born to freedom, in its natural ignorance, and to lay the foundation of improvement in free inquiries, with a disposition to yield to the evidence of truth, wherever we could find it. If, thus prepared, we have attained to any knowledge in the art of speaking, if our language be at all polished, if words drop from our tongues with smooth and easy cadence (suffer us to own it) to you we owe it. If our reasoning powers be at all improved, if we can think, if we can judge or reason justly of things, or, if lastly, we can cast our thoughts into a proper method and range them in just order, this is an evidence of your own skill in these things and of your faithfull care in teaching us. And how often, dear sir, have you surprised our minds with discoveries in the mysteries of number and mathematical quantity? And how often the demonstration of the mutual habitudes, proportions and ratios of things richly compensate our close application and labor to know them. But 'tis with painfull steps we follow your adventrous, generous, towering abroad in the wide expanse of nature. We are sometimes struck with amazement at the power of the Almighty Creatour, in obedience to whose Omnipotent word the mighty frame arose out of nothing, by whom these Heavens were stretched out and bespangled with their golden glories. We are [^012-note3] sometimes also struck with admiration of the wisdom and skill of the great Establisher, when we consider the law by which He governs all in exact harmony and regular order, admiring the beautiful proportions observed now in the motions, now in the distances and now in the magnitudes of the globes that compose this our system, the sun (the foundation of light and heat) fixt in the middle by whose constant emitted rays the circumvolving planets, primary and secondary, are cherished and warmed, and to whom with prone descent they tend as to their center, but happily controlled by a contrary force in beautiful concert, they go their eternal round. We discover the evident tokens of superior goodness, wisdom and power of the creating, preserving and governing spirit, all the way downward till our tho'ts again light on this our earth, where all creation also join their universal voice to proclaim the being and excellencies of the Creatour. Here you have led us into the deepest recesses of nature, and disclosed her most intricate contrivances. To you, dear sir, we owe, we acknoledge, our best acquirements. Our sincerest affections to, and heartiest wishes for your happiness will never cease to move us in all possible proper methods and well contrived ways to expect it; your best deserts of us this [^012-note4] demand this debt; we rejoice, dear sir, that the commonwealth of learning has been so far promoted by your efficacious efforts; we rejoice that this dear seat of the muses has also been yours, that you have happily promoted the sacred sciences and generous arts here cultivated. The fruit of your labors will transmit your name thro' the eternal successions of [^012-note5] posterity precious. May adversity or misery never know you. May you grow a greater ornament of letters, a greater honour of your country (to the service of which we are glad you are deservedly promoted), a greater beauty and delight of all yours. May the due rewards of heroick virtue be yours. May you at last be possessed of eternal bliss. Farewell!

[Continues with tributes to Whitman and Hubbard, and concludes with exhortation to the Scholars.]

[^012-note3]: **marginal.** [24]

[^012-note5]: **marginal.** [25]

## Receipt for Slave Named Venus Receipt for Venus

AnonymousReceipt for Slave "Venus," 6/7/31. BRBL, in three parts: 1) Box 14, f. 1156; 2) sermon on Ez. 44:9, 1749 (between pp. 8 & 10) and 3) sermon on Is. 30:20 f., Mar. 1750 (pp. 2-3)

KNOW ALL MEN by these presents That I Richard Perkins of Newport in the County of Newport & Colony of Rhode Island &c Marriner For & in Consideration of the Sum of Eighty pounds of lawful Current money of said Colony To me in hand well & truly paid at & before the ensealing & delivery hereof by Jonathan Edwards of Northampton in the County of Hampshire & Province of the Massachusetts Bay in New England Clerk The receipt whereof I do hereby acknowledge and thereof & of every part & parcel thereof do exonerate aquit & discharge the said Jonathan Edwards his heirs Execrs Admrs & Assigns by these presents HAVE bargained sold & delivered And I the said Richard Perkins do hereby bargain sell & deliver unto the said Jonathan Edwards a Negro Girle named Venus aged Fourteen years or thereabout TO HAVE & TO HOLD the said Negro girl named Venus unto the said Jonathan Edwards his heirs Execrs & Assigns and to his & their own proper Use & behoof for Ever AND I the said Richard Perkins do hereby for my Self my heirs Execrs & Admrs covenant promise & agree to & with the said Jonathan Edwards his heirs Execrs Admrs & Assigns by these presents That I the said Richard Perkins at the ensealing & delivery hereof have in my own Name good Right, full Power & lawfull Authority to bargain sell & deliver the said Negro Girl named Venus unto the said Jonathan Edwards in manner & form aforesaid And shall & will warrant & defend the said Negro Girle named Venus unto the said Jonathan Edwards his heirs Execrs Admrs & Assigns against the lawfull Challenge & Demand of all manner of Persons whatsoever Claiming or to claim by from or under me or otherwise howsoever IN WITNESS whereof I the said Richard Perkins have hereunto set my hand & Seal the Seventh day of June in the Fourth Year of the Reign of our Soveraign Lord George the Second by the grace of God of Great Britain France & Ireland King Defender of the Faith &c

Anno Dm 1731

Richd Perkins

Sealed & Delivered in the presence of us

John Cranston

Jas Martin

Att his Majy Court of General Sessions of the Peace Holden at Northampton by Adjournment within & for the County of Hampshire on the Third Tuesday of January Anno Dome 1735/6 Being the Ninth Year of the Reign of Our Sove- raign Lord George the Second by the Grace of God of Great Brittain France & Ireland King Defendr of the faith &c

The Grandjurors of our sd Lord the King within & for the Body of the County of Hampshire Do on their Oath present that Barnard Bartlet Sometime Since of Simsberry In the County of Hartford & Collony of Connecticut Since Resident In Northampton Aforesd Husbandman Did on or About the Sixth Day of December Last Past in Northampton Aforesd> Wittingly & Willingly make & publish a Libel Tending to the Defamation of the Rev.d Mr Jonth Edwards of Sd Northampton Clerk by Saying that the Sd Mr Edwards was as Great an Instrument as the Devil Had on this Side Hell to bring Souls to Hell which Sd Speach of Sd Bartlet is Contrary to Law the Peace of Our Sd Lord the King &c

Witnesses

## Valedictory Oration Valedictory Oration

Jonathan EdwardsValedictory Oration (introduced and translated by George Levesque)

Although Jonathan Edwards is undoubtedly one of Yale's most distinguished graduates, the fledgling "Collegiate School" that welcomed him in the fall of 1716 was itself hardly distinguished. The college, founded in 1701, was scarcely older than the thirteen-year-old, and given its fragile condition it was not clear whether it would survive long enough to graduate him. The Class of 1716 numbered only three students, and despite constant efforts from the trustees, the college still had no building, no fixed location, and no full-time rector.[^015-note1] The rector *pro tempore* was one of the trustees, Samuel Andrew, but he was a busy minister who continued his pastoral duties in Milford, while the students and tutors met in various homes in Saybrook, some forty miles east along the shoreline. This arrangement had lasted for almost ten years, since the death of the first rector, Abraham Pierson, but students and parents complained often about the inconsistent instruction and minimal oversight, and they talked more than once about withdrawing from the school. Complaints about the tutors had grown so loud, in fact, that the trustees had to "quiet the Schollars" by temporarily allowing them to "repair to such instructors as they might be satisfied in."[^015-note2] Some students went to their homes, while a group of about ten gathered in Wethersfield, just south of Hartford, under the direction of independent tutors.

To make matters worse for the fragile college, the trustees themselves became increasingly divided. The main point of dispute was the permanent location of the college. Although the trustees had originally selected Saybrook as the site for the college, the first two rectors never lived there, so the school had followed a nomadic existence for its first fifteen years, meeting in various homes and towns along the shoreline. Given their lack of success in recruiting a rector for Saybrook, trustees with strong connections to either Hartford or New Haven, then co-capitals of the Connecticut Colony, lobbied to move the college, with each camp supporting its own community. Meanwhile, other trustees maintained that Saybrook, at the mouth of the Connecticut River and free from the political maneuverings of Hartford or New Haven, was still the ideal location.

As the new academic year began in the fall of 1716, the matter remained unresolved. Unable to reach consensus on a single location, the trustees compromised and allowed students to choose among *three* locations: Saybrook, Wethersfield, or New Haven. Before young Jonathan could set off for college, therefore, his father, the Rev. Timothy Edwards, first had to decide which branch of the Collegiate School his son would attend. For the Edwards family, it was an easy choice. Wethersfield was just on the other side of Hartford from their home in East Windsor, only ten miles down the Connecticut River, and Jonathan's father liked the idea of keeping his only son nearby. Moreover, the primary tutor for the Wethersfield students, Elisha Williams, and the town's minister, Stephen Mix, were both Edwards relatives on the distinguished Stoddard side of the family. Although Jonathan could not be sure where, how, or if he would complete his collegiate education, he nevertheless had good reason to hope that Wethersfield would provide a safe and solid start.

Throughout the 1716-1717 academic year, students remained in their respective locations as the trustees continued to debate the permanent location of the college. By the fall of 1717, it finally appeared that a resolution was on the horizon, and the advocates for New Haven had slowly emerged as the victors. The September Commencement exercises were held for the first time in the meetinghouse on the New Haven Green, and a few weeks later the New Haven trustees were able to purchase, with the assistance of local donations, a tract of land across from the Green as a site for the college. In their haste to secure New Haven as a permanent location, they immediately began construction on a building. The Hartford trustees continued to dissent and appealed their cause to the Connecticut General Assembly, but after many rounds of debate, the Assembly ruled on behalf of the majority New Haven trustees. Refusing to give up their fight, the dissenting trustees nevertheless persuaded the Wethersfield students, including Edwards, to remain separate in hope that they might persuade the Assembly to reconsider their ruling.[^015-note3]

Despite the obstinacy of the upriver students and trustees, the New Haven trustees nevertheless moved forward with their plans, and as they watched the new college building take shape, they grew ever more confident of their ultimate victory. The new building would not only secure New Haven as the permanent site for the college, but it would also finally provide a home for an extensive library that Jeremiah Dummer, a patron of the college and colonial agent in London, had collected and donated almost five years earlier. In a letter to Dummer, the trustees exulted in their progress:

The affair of or. School hath been in a Condition of Pregnancy. Painfull with a witness have been the Throwes thereof in this General Assembly. But we just now hear, that after the Violent Pangs threatening the Very life of the Babe, Divine Providence as a kind Obstetrix hath mercifully brought forth the Babe into the World, & behold a Man-child is born, whereat We all Rejoyce.[^015-note4]

Encouraged by this news, Dummer and Cotton Mather, the disaffected former president of Harvard and early supporter of the Connecticut college, secured a generous benefaction from a wealthy Welsh merchant, Elihu Yale, to help the struggling Collegiate School. Yale's grandmother, father, and uncle were among the earliest settlers of New Haven, so the college's new location undoubtedly made the pitch from Dummer and Mather more compelling. And with a building going up, Mather suggested, Yale could finance the completion of the structure that would commemorate him "better than an Egyptian pyramid."[^015-note5] Yale apparently liked the idea and soon shipped some books and a large box of valuable goods from East India, which the trustees sold in Boston for a handsome profit. In grateful recognition of the gift, the trustees voted at the Commencement of 1718 to call the new building "Yale Colledge," a name that soon became synonymous for the whole school.[^015-note6]

As the trustees and townspeople in New Haven joyously celebrated Commencement and the commemoration of the college's first building, Edwards and the other students in Wethersfield carried on separately. The Hartford trustees even held a rival commencement in Wethersfield, awarding degrees to five students. But the trustees soon realized that they could not continue their independence for much longer and compete with New Haven's growing resources and support. Moreover, the General Assembly had grown weary of the dissension, and they again intervened. They passed "An Act for Encouragement of Yale College" that granted Hartford £500 for a new courthouse if the town gave up its fight to relocate the college. The Hartford trustees realized they could fight no longer. By late November, Jonathan and the other Wethersfield students had packed up their belongings and at last dutifully reported to New Haven.[^015-note7] Finally, all the students were united in one location.

One significant problem remained, however: the college still lacked a resident rector. The trustees were nevertheless confident that, with a permanent location and building, they could quickly recruit one. For the present, however, the students remained under the charge of tutors, an arrangement that soon caused familiar problems. Shortly after their arrival in New Haven, the students grew dissatisfied and drew up a "Collection of faults" they found with "the public Expositions & Disputations & managemts. of the Tutrs.."[^015-note8] As Jonathan later explained to his sister, Mary Edwards, the chief complaint was against tutor Samuel Johnson.[^015-note9] The stated reasons for their dissatisfaction concerned Johnson's teaching, though it is doubtful that their complaints were with his intellectual abilities. He was one of the college's brightest graduates (Class of 1714) and later became the founding president of King's College (Columbia). A more probable cause for their discontent was that Johnson's rationalistic theological leanings, which eventually led him to Anglicanism, differed from the evangelical "experimental religion" of the upriver students. It is also likely that political forces were still at work as the Hartford trustees continued to exert their influence over the Wethersfield students. Whatever the precise reasons, Jonathan and the other Wethersfield students again packed up their belongings after only one month's stay in New Haven and resumed their studies with Elisha Williams in January 1719. The "violent pangs" of birth were obviously not yet over. [^015-note10]

With the college again divided, colonial officials tried once more to mediate. Instead of the General Assembly, however, this time Governor Saltonstall and his council met privately with the trustees, that "having a right understanding of the management of the said deserters, which had occasioned so much disorder, proper measures might be taken to redress what should be found amiss, and prevent the like for the future."[^015-note11] Rector Andrew explained that the only stated cause of the desertion was "the insufficiency of one of the tutors," but he did not doubt that the complaints against Johnson's services "would be found, upon enquiry, most unreasonable."[^015-note12] Other trustees confirmed this assessment, and the council members were satisfied. The real problem, the trustees and the council agreed, was that the college still lacked a resident rector. The trustees were happy to report, however, that they had recently found a promising candidate: Timothy Cutler, pastor of the Canterbury parish in Stratford. The governor and council were relieved by the news and hoped a permanent solution was finally in sight. If the new rector proved satisfactory, the Wethersfield contingent would have no more cause to complain, and the Assembly could therefore order them to return to New Haven.

Cutler arrived in New Haven a few weeks later, and when the news of his appointment reached Wethersfield in March, the students there were indeed pleased and willing to return to New Haven. Jonathan wrote to his sister Mary that "the council and trustees…have put in Mr. Cutler, Pastor of Canterbury, President who (as we hear) intends very speedily to be resident at Yale College, so that all the scholars belonging to our school expect to return there, as soon as our vacancy after the election is over."[^015-note13] At last, the long labor to "erect a collegiate school" appeared complete. After almost two decades of uncertainty and nomadic existence, the college had an established location, a permanent building, and a resident rector. And after three years of uncertainty, Jonathan could finally begin his senior year, no longer a pawn in political debates.

Despite the frequent interruptions, Jonathan's studies did not appear to suffer; in fact, he seemed to thrive even under the challenging circumstances. Although the upriver trustees failed in their attempt to move the college, they found an excellent leader and tutor in Elisha Williams, later president of the college, and were able to employ two other tutors, providing the Wethersfield students with more tutors than either Saybrook or New Haven. Now in New Haven, Jonathan and all the seniors came under the direct oversight of the new rector, Timothy Cutler. Cutler already knew Timothy Edwards, his fellow Connecticut clergyman, and wrote him shortly after Jonathan arrived in New Haven, commending his son's "promising ability and advances in learning" and assuring him that Jonathan would be under the rector's watchful care.[^015-note14] Timothy Edwards later wrote Cutler, hoping that his son would behave "discreetly and soberly" and make the most of "his prsent Opportunity at ye College… Considering the time that he hath Lost and how Near it is to its end."[^015-note15] Jonathan, for his part, was apparently happy in his new environment. After a month in New Haven, he wrote his father, "I take very great content under my present tuition as all the rest of the scholars seem to do under theirs. Mr. Cutler is extraordinarily courteous to us, has a very good spirit of government, keeps the school in excellent order, seems to increase in learning, and is loved and feared by all that are under him."[^015-note16] As evidence of Jonathan's success, the trustees appointed him as the college butler for his senior year, a paid position that involved serving bread and beer to the students at meals and assisting the college steward with the financial accounts.[^015-note17]

In addition to the benefits of a resident rector and a new building, the students in New Haven now enjoyed the long-awaited fruits of the college library. For almost five years, since Dummer's first donation of books in 1714, the library had remained in boxed storage at the home of Justice Daniel Buckingham in Saybrook, where the volumes served as valuable bargaining chips in the political debates over the location of the college. After the college house was built in New Haven, the Governor's Council ordered that the books be moved to the new location, but the county sheriff still had to remove the books forcibly from Buckingham's house. Even as the caravan proceeded to New Haven, an unruly mob of town residents overturned the carts, turned loose the oxen, and damaged the bridges from Saybrook to New Haven to prevent the delivery. It took three days to make the thirty-mile journey, and over 250 books were lost, but the remaining one thousand books eventually arrived safely.[^015-note18]

The books were well worth the wait and the fight. Among the volumes in the Dummer collection were not only the major theological writings of the day but also the latest scientific and philosophical works from abroad, including works of Isaac Newton and John Locke, which surely thrilled a preternaturally inquisitive student like Jonathan. Although Edwards scholars continue to debate when he first encountered some of these works, and how much they influenced his later thought, Edwards almost surely had some access to them through the college library by his senior year in New Haven. Inspired perhaps by some of Newton's works, Jonathan likely began to compose some of his precocious scientific observations during this time, including his famous essay "Of Insects," the first of Edwards's "Spider" papers.[^015-note19] Jonathan's most legendary, though not necessarily most important, intellectual encounter, however, was with Locke. Edwards's first biographer, Samuel Hopkins, famously described Jonathan's reading *Essay Concerning Human Understanding* "with great delight and profit" in college and reported that Edwards took the book in his hands shortly before his death, commenting that he had "more Satisfaction and Pleasure in studying it, than the most greedy Miser in gathering up handsful of Silver and Gold from some new discover'd Treasure." [^015-note20]

Jonathan's reading of modern thinkers supplemented the traditional curriculum of the college, still strongly steeped in the medieval educational formula of the *trivium* (grammar, logic, and rhetoric) and *quadrivium* (algebra, geometry, astronomy, and music), which was itself a Christian reformulation of ancient Greek and Roman education. Following the pattern of Harvard College and its English ancestors, the instructors at Yale inoculated the largely pagan curriculum with regular doses of prayer and theological study, chiefly through William Ames's *Medulla Theologiae* and *Cases of Conscience*, Johann Wollebius's *Compendium Theologiae Christianae*, and the Westminster Assembly's *Shorter Catechism*. Inasmuch as Yale's stated mission was to produce a "succession of Learned & Orthodox men," solid learning and sound theology were the daily diet, enforced through a strict daily schedule of study, scripture reading, and public worship.[^015-note21]

The intense regimen seems to have suited Jonathan's highly introspective and disciplined nature. As his graduation in the fall of 1720 approached, he was at the head of his class and was therefore selected to deliver an oration at Commencement.[^015-note22] This honor surely elicited equal amounts of anxiety and excitement in the habitually nervous and ambitious young scholar, for though college commencements were festive occasions, they were also public examinations, designed ostensibly to allow the trustees and the general educated public to verify the learning of the new graduates. This "Public Act" required each of the degree candidates to prepare answers to questions, called *Theses*, in various branches of knowledge (*Logicae*, *Grammaticae*, *Mathematicae*, etc.), which they then "manfully defended" (*pro virili defendendae*) during the commencement exercises. The *Theses* and the names of the graduates were printed in advance on a broadsheet and distributed to the audience so that they could follow the proceedings and interject if so desired. Jonathan, not yet even seventeen years old, and on public display for the first time, would therefore be expected to prepare both his assigned *Theses* and a valedictory address. As a further challenge, he would of course need to compose his disputations and address in Latin, the language of instruction in the college.[^015-note23]

Details of the Commencement exercises at Yale during this time are sketchy, but as with many things, the college seemed to follow the pattern of Harvard. Cotton Mather, president of Harvard from 1692 to 1701, described Commencement Days in Cambridge "whither the Magistrates and Ministers, and other Gentlemen then came, to put Respect upon their Exercises." The exercises included "Orations both Salutary and Valedictory, made by some or other of the Commencers…. But the main Exercises were Disputations upon Questions, wherein the Respondents first made their Theses."[^015-note24] Thomas Clap, president of Yale from 1740-1766, later described Yale commencements during his presidency, and little seems to have changed from Mather's day. Clap writes:

The publick Commencement is ordinarily on the second Wednesday in September annually: at which there is a large Assembly, consisting of the President and Fellows, a great Number of Ministers, and other learned and superior Gentlemen. The President begins the Solemnity with Prayer, one of the Candidates, for the first Degree, makes a salutary Oration to the Governor and Council, the Officers of the College, and the whole Assembly: the others give a Specimen of their Learning, by disputing Syllogistically on the Questions, printed in their theses; which are then distributed. The like is done in the Afternoon by the Candidates for the Degree Master of Arts. Then the President, with the Consent of the Fellows, gives them their Degrees…. Then one of the Masters makes a Valedictory Oration: and the President concludes the whole Solemnity with Prayer."[^015-note25]

According to Clap's description, and from what we know of contemporary practices at Harvard, the "Valedictory Oration" was ordinarily reserved for one of the M.A. candidates, so it is not clear if Edwards delivered *the* valedictory address for some special reason, or if in fact he delivered *one* *of several* valedictory addresses, what Clap called the "salutary Oration."[^015-note26] In any case, it was a great honor to speak at Commencement, and Jonathan seems to recognize both the privilege and burden of delivering the address. It is, however, difficult to distinguish his true sentiments from the flowery formality of his language. On the one hand, he possesses "a mind endowed with fortunate productiveness, furnished with eloquence and greatly equipped with expert knowledge," yet on the other hand he is "gripped by an inability to speak and by the greatest sense of wonder." He is tempted to flee from this duty in fear that he will disgrace himself, dishonor the school, and fail to express adequately the gratitude and good-byes of his fellow graduates. He nevertheless entrusts himself to the audience's generosity and "with trembling tongue and palpitating heart" undertakes his task, "doubtful of success."

The rest of his address is largely a panegyric to the faculty and friends of the college. He begins with Timothy Cutler, the long-awaited "Rector of highest distinction," whom Edwards describes as "the perennial fountain of the body, the life and motion of this School, the source of its living spirits, for which we have constantly been waiting and to which we have everyday been disposed." With Cutler at last in place, he says, the college has been "brought so suddenly from its infancy to its perfection." He next praises Warham Mather, Justice of the Peace in New Haven, who helped to oversee the construction of college's first building, and the Rev. Joseph Noyes, an alumnus (Class of 1709), former tutor, and present minister of the Church of Christ in New Haven. Lastly, he thanks Daniel Brown (Class of 1714), "outstanding tutor of this Academy," whose labors "unceasingly brought benefits" to his students. He concludes finally with fond farewells to his fellow students, "for that pleasing fellowship by which we have been refreshed," whose faces will always remain in everyone's mind "as if carved from marble or sculpted from bronze." As his parting valediction, he proclaims, "Farewell to you celebrated guides and farewell to our fellow students, lovers of learning. May all those always present in their scholarship fare well." [^015-note27]

Following his graduation, Edwards decided to remain in New Haven to read for his M.A. degree, an undertaking that ordinarily required three years. Unlike the course of study for the B.A., however, the trustees did not prescribe a set curriculum for the M.A. Instead, the candidates were simply expected to spend time in their own scholarly pursuits. After this period of study, the candidate would then present "a written synopsis either of logick or naturall phylosophy or metaphysicks as also a Common place on some Divinity theses and ye solution of two or three problems such as ye Rector shall propose to him and be approved by ye trustees att Commencement."[^015-note28] Without a fixed curriculum, no residency was required, so only a few bachelors remained in New Haven for their studies.[^015-note29] Those who did remain at college, like Edwards, were usually preparing for the ministry and often combined their study with a pastoral apprenticeship and regular pulpit supply.

During his first year of graduate study, Edwards continued his duties as the college butler and lived on campus with his cousin Elisha Mix, apparently at the urging of his father and uncle who wanted Jonathan to look after his younger cousin who was just beginning at Yale. Elisha was the son of the Rev. Stephen Mix from Wethersfield, so they knew each other well from Jonathan's earlier days there, but it seems that the new chambermates soon resented this arrangement. Elisha grumbled at Jonathan's constant oversight and nagging, and Jonathan objected to Elisha's indolence and recalcitrance. It was not a happy union, and Jonathan wrote his uncle after just a few weeks to complain. It seems that Elisha was not an earnest student and resisted his chores because he did not "Love to be Called from play to Draw Cyder." Jonathan could not comprehend such absence of discipline and disrespect of authority, and he quickly lost patience. Given Jonathan's personality, one could imagine that he was an exacting and exasperating guardian, but Elisha was also more than a little unruly, and Jonathan persuaded his uncle to release him from his obligations.[^015-note30]

Later in that academic year, in March 1721, we again see Jonathan's precocious and priggish side when a student rebellion erupted concerning food in the "college Commons." The disruption had apparently gained some negative publicity in the colony, and his father inquired about it. Jonathan replied and explained that the undergraduates "all on a sudden, Before Mr Cutler, or (I Believe) any Body Knew yt they were Discontented, entered into a Bond of 15s never to have any More Commons of the Steward." Their boycott brought swift action from the "exceedingly Vex'd and Displeased" Rector Cutler and several of the trustees who immediately demanded the students to return. The "affrighted" students complied, but they "so lost Mr Cutler's favour that they scarce ever will Regain it." Jonathan was quick to assure his father that he lent no credence to "this Cabal." "I must needs say for my Own part," he wrote, "that although the Commons at sometimes have not been sufficient as to quality, yet I think there has been very little Occasion for such an insurrection as this." Jonathan went on to report other disturbances, "Much Worse, and Greater, and I believe Greater than Ever were in the Colledge before." Among the "Monstrous impieties, and acts of Immorality Lately Committed," he writes, were "stealing of Hens, Geese, turkies, piggs, Meat Wood &c-, Unseasonable Nightwalking, Breaking People's windows, playing at Cards, Cursing, Swearing, and Damning, and Using all manner of Ill language." Again, Jonathan was quick to report that he had nothing to do with these actions-"Through the goodness of God I am perfectly free of all their janglings"-and was hopeful that the trustees would properly respond to these kinds of actions.[^015-note31]

As a resident graduate student and as the college butler, Edwards was distinct from the rest of the students and was in some position of authority, but he was the same age as many of the undergraduates, if not younger. Such moral indignation from a peer was undoubtedly hard to receive, and it is not surprising that Edwards struggled with many of his personal relationships in college. As George Marsden suggests, however, Jonathan's difficulties with fellow students and his harsh judgment of others may reflect more of his own spiritual journey, which was then at its most intense stage, than his lack of charity. According to his "Personal Narrative," Edwards was at this time, "in the midst of many uneasy thoughts" about his spiritual state. The son of a minister, he had since childhood wanted desperately to experience the "sweet delight in God and divine things," but he could not overcome "objections against the doctrine of God's sovereignty, " which remained a "horrible doctrine" to him.[^015-note32] Meanwhile, the inconsistencies of his own obedience to God's commands and the immoral behavior of his fellow students at college, presumably unlike anything he had seen before, deeply troubled him. He was, as Marsden explains, "living like a young monk seeking sainthood in a school of rowdy boys."[^015-note33]

Sometime during the early spring of 1721, however, Edwards had a spiritual breakthrough. As he again explains in the "Personal Narrative," he began to have new "apprehensions and ideas of Christ, and the work of redemption, and the glorious way of salvation by him." As he read and meditated upon Scripture, he says, "The sense I had of divine things, would often of a sudden as it were, kindle up a sweet burning in my heart; an ardor of my soul, that I know not how to express." Shortly thereafter, while home for a brief vacation, Jonathan had a long conversation with his father in which he "gave an account" of his recent contemplations and was "pretty much affected by the discourse." Later, as he walked alone in his father's pasture, he was again enraptured by "a sweet sense of the glorious majesty and grace of God." For the first time, he recounted, he was able to see God's "majesty and meekness joined together: it was a sweet and gentle, and holy majesty; and also a majestic meekness; an awful sweetness; a high, and great, and holy gentleness." At last, the "horrible doctrine" of God's sovereignty had become "an exceedingly pleasant, bright and sweet doctrine."[^015-note34]

The following fall, in August 1722, still glowing in his new "sense of divine things," Edwards accepted an invitation to supply the pulpit for a small Presbyterian congregation in New York City. Not yet ordained, this was an occasion for Jonathan to test his preaching skills and confirm his call to the ministry. One member of the congregation, John Smith, had graduated a year ahead of him at Yale, and Edwards lived in the city with Smith and his "pious mother." He spent eight months of "sweet and pleasant days" in New York, apparently some of the happiest days of his life, but by spring, it was time to leave. Timothy Edwards was eager to have his son back in Connecticut and had arranged a potential pastoral call for Jonathan in the town of Bolton, only fifteen miles from East Windsor. After a "most bitter parting with Madam Smith and her son," Jonathan obediently returned home to pursue his candidacy for the Bolton pulpit and complete the requirements for his M.A. degree.[^015-note35]

Back in East Windsor, Edwards spent the summer preparing for the Bolton pastorate and completing his "written synopsis" for public delivery at the upcoming Yale Commencement of 1723, scheduled for September 20. Similar to the bachelor's *Theses*, the master's disputations, called *Quaestiones*, were more complicated and required a substantial discourse. The oral delivery of a *Quaestio* was largely a formality, however, and there was little doubt that Edwards would pass this test, yet he still confesses much anxiety in his diary entries over the summer. He mentions, for example, concerns about performing something for "public acceptance" surrounding a future "event" with potentially "adverse" circumstances.[^015-note36] We cannot know for sure what was on his mind, but it seems likely that his upcoming Commencement address somehow contributed to his anxieties. He was apparently not eagerly looking forward to the pastoral call in Bolton that his father had arranged, so perhaps he saw his M.A. disputation as one last opportunity to impress either the college trustees who might be looking for a tutor or church representatives from a more prestigious congregation looking for a young pastor.[^015-note37]

Whatever Jonathan's concerns were that summer, he was conscious at least of one thing as he prepared his disputation: more eyes than usual would be watching this particular Commencement, for the whole colony was still talking about what had happened a year earlier at the Commencement of 1722. Just as the young college seemed at last to be thriving, it faced its greatest crisis yet. Rector Timothy Cutler, the long-awaited leader expected finally to establish the young college, turned out to be a heretic. At the close of the Commencement exercises, he announced his conversion to Anglicanism, made plain by the conspicuous conclusion of his final prayer straight from the Book of Common Prayer-"and let all the people say, Amen."[^015-note38] With these words, six other men in the audience, including one of the Yale tutors, joined Cutler in his announcement, apparently by previous agreement.[^015-note39]

The seriousness of this defection may be lost to modern readers, but for Puritan New England, their actions were tantamount to high treason. It was, of course, corruption in the Church of England that the Dissenters had hoped to "purify" by their removal to New England, so the thought that the enemy was in their own backyard was a devastating blow. Among their strongest objections to Anglicanism, particularly by the eighteenth century, was the growing influence of Arminianism, which held a relatively positive view of human reason and will, including the ability to receive or reject God's grace. For strict Calvinists who believed that God was completely sovereign in salvation, Arminianism was the first step toward rationalistic religion and Unitarianism. Such theological pollution had long been evident up at Harvard, but now many feared that Yale was in even greater danger.

News of this shocking announcement spread throughout New England, and it soon became known as "The Great Apostacie." The defection was all the more surprising and disturbing because Cutler had been widely regarded as a model defender of Puritan convictions. In fact, he was originally recruited to Connecticut to counteract Anglicanism, which was gaining a foothold along the southwest shoreline of the state. This was the man whom Edwards described only two years earlier in his valedictory address as the college's "perennial fountain…the source of its living spirits, for which we have constantly been waiting and to which we have everyday been disposed." The fountain, it turned out, was poisoned. Equally alarming, the six other men who simultaneously announced their conversion were all Yale graduates, and three had taught there in significant capacities.[^015-note40] Had the very nursery they established to promote orthodoxy become a cradle of heresy? Major James Fitch, one of the college's benefactors, lamented the defection in verse:

And is it not to our Sorrow a Great Aggravation, That such in our College had their Education? That out of that Mother of Learning did spring That should her Sons and Churches mortally sting.[^015-note41]

As Edwards prepared his disputation, therefore, he was surely conscious that the audience would scrutinize his words, carefully listening for any hint of heterodoxy. In no small measure, the reputation of Yale, and with it the hopes and fears of the Connecticut colony, was at stake. Edwards, characteristically, did not back down from the challenge. Although he was qualified to "dispute" on a wide range of topics, including those related to his detailed scientific observations, Edwards boldly selected for his *Quaestio* a theological topic at the center of the recent controversies. He defended the classic Reformation doctrine of justification by faith in Christ alone, phrasing his *Quaestio* this way: "A sinner is not justified in the sight of God, except through the righteousness of Christ obtained by faith." Following the standard methods of scholastic disputation, Edwards did not rely upon Scripture-proofs but argued exclusively from the "mutual consistency of God's attributes" and the laws of non-contradiction. Edwards's main point was to demonstrate that his opponents, in suggesting that sinners are justified through "sincere obedience," were setting up a new form of works righteousness and a false system of justification. The logical implication of their position, Edwards claimed, was that a "sinner is his own redeemer." Everyone in the audience doubtless recognized his address as a frontal assault on the Arminian components of the recent "Apostacie." They also quickly perceived that the young Yale graduate had a bright future as a defender of the faith.[^015-note42]

Following Commencement, Edwards returned home to make final preparations for his move to Bolton to begin his new pastorate. Yet leaving New Haven behind was hardly easy. In addition to the intellectual stimulation of the college, Edwards would also have to part with a young lady whom he fancied, Sarah Pierpont, his future wife. She was the daughter of the late James Pierpont, long-time minister of the First Church in New Haven, where Edwards attended services, and the brother of James Pierpont, Jr., one of the Yale tutors. Edwards had likely known her for several years and presumably saw her again on his trip to New Haven for Commencement. She was still young, just thirteen years old, but to Edwards she was the epitome of Christian virtue and beauty, as his famous ode to her, written at about this time, demonstrates. "They say there is young lady in [New Haven]," he marveled, "who is beloved of that almighty Being, who made and rules the world, and that there are certain seasons in which this great Being, in some way or other invisible, comes to her and fills her mind with exceeding sweet delight, and that she hardly cares for anything, except to meditate on him."[^015-note43] With thoughts of Sarah and New Haven in his mind, Edwards nevertheless duly reported to Bolton in early November and took up his pastoral responsibilities.

After only a few months in Bolton, however, Edwards was rejoicing in a turn of providence that would send him back to New Haven. In the spring of 1724, Sarah's brother resigned his tutorship at Yale, creating a vacancy at the college. Edwards had already expressed strong interest in the position, and the trustees knew that he was highly qualified, so in May they offered him an appointment.[^015-note44] Just a few weeks later, Edwards eagerly returned to New Haven and began his new duties. His enthusiasm for the job quickly turned to discouragement, however, for the college was even less orderly than when he last resided on campus two years earlier as a graduate student. Following Cutler's defection, Yale had difficulty recruiting a resident rector, and the day-to-day administration of the college fell once again to the tutors. Edwards and another new tutor were therefore responsible for running the college as the trustees continued their search for a permanent rector. In addition to providing instruction in every subject, he and his fellow tutor were in charge of the welfare and discipline of all the students.[^015-note45] Given Jonathan's earlier impatience with his cousin, we can only imagine how overwhelmed he was by the more than fifty students under their charge. After only one week in his post, and perhaps seeing in his students the effects of the Fall in new light, he confessed in his diary, "I have now abundant reason to be convinced of the troublesomeness and vexation of the world, and that it never will be another kind of world."[^015-note46]

Edwards's tenure as a tutor at Yale was difficult in almost every way. By the end of his second year, he was physically exhausted, emotionally drained, and spiritually starved. On one trip back home to East Windsor for vacation, in fact, he was so tired and ill that he collapsed on the way and spent three months recuperating in North Haven before he returned to Yale. Most troubling for him, characteristically, was his spiritual state. He later recounted in his diary, reflecting on his time as a tutor, that he had been "for the most part in a low, sunk estate and condition, miserably senseless to what I used to be, about spiritual things."[^015-note47]

Despite the many challenges and heavy demands of being a tutor, at least his duties did not lack intellectual stimulation. Ever the polymath, Edwards no doubt enjoyed teaching a wide range of subjects to those students who were eager to learn. He also likely formed a close friendship with one of the M.A. students in residence at the time, Gilbert Tennent, later a prominent revivalist and trusted ally of Edwards. Even one of his most tedious tasks, sorting and cataloguing all the books of the library, surely brought some satisfaction and welcome relief from his daily grind.[^015-note48] He nevertheless realized it was time to move on.

As Edwards pondered his next step, providence again opened a door. In the spring of 1726, it was announced that the legendary Solomon Stoddard, Jonathan's grandfather and the long-time pastor of the church in Northampton, Massachusetts, needed an assistant. Now in his eighties, Stoddard could no longer meet single-handedly the demands of the congregation, which was the largest in New England outside of Boston. The church hoped to recruit an able associate to help Stoddard as needed, and the obvious implication was that their aging pastor would soon retire and pass on the reigns of this prominent parish. Stoddard no doubt recommended his bright grandson, and the congregation subsequently invited him to preach. Being favorably impressed with Edwards, they voted to extend him a call in the fall of 1726, and he accepted. With his formal education and training at Yale completed, despite the many twists and turns, he was now equipped and ready to begin his remarkable public career as preacher, pastor, philosopher, and theologian.

*****

The manuscript of the Valedictory is five long duodecimo leaves, the size Edwards commonly used for his sermons early in his career. The bottoms of the second and third leaves are torn away, creating signficant lacunae.

Oratio Valedictoria

Habita

In Aula Accademica.

Quando meipsum Locatum, et Locuturum, Viri audientes Amplissimi, in medio Conventus, in medio Cujus, maxima Doctrina abùndat, Scientia summa habitat, virtus immaculata Refulget; quando Oratione me Collaturum vobis Animadverto, Quod Vir solum mente Possessus, facunditate Laute Indutâ, eloquentiâ Præ ditâ, et solertiâ ter Accinctâ, Dignus est Peragere, Repentè Stupore et Maximâ miratione, Obsideor, et a timore transfigor; sicut atque meipso territum meum Oculum (quod vix audeo facere) inverto, verbis Anima mea maximè Horret: Sicut vobis iterùm extra me Cogitationes undiq; Peragitatas (que quoque terrefio) reverto, nunc quidem nihili audax; Heu! Conditio mea! me quaquaversùm tremor Circumdat; Jam me inertiæ ineptiæq; Condemno; Hoc loco abesse me volo ac Super His Rostris virum adesse, qui Literis Huic Atq; Scholæ (quam Potissimùm ejus esse Sedem Gloriamur) non De±de±cus fuerit.

Qui quidem dignus se esset vobis Præstare Orantem, qui eloquiis eloquentiæ non hebesceret aciem, qui Cùm parem Gratitudinem, tùm Gratitudinis Causam Potuit edere. Qui Deniquè nostrùm Optationes, nostrûm averturorum aversationes, nostrûm omnes Cogitationes valedicentes, profectò [^015-note49] potuit enarrare: sed Hisce Omnibus ego Destitutus, Clementiæ vestræ Contemplationibus excitatus, eâq; Omninò fretus, Licet Linguâ tremulâ, Corde Palpitanti, frustrà Hoc negotium suscepi: Obsecro itaque, siquidèm hæc res non fugienda, vobis orati me propitii Condonetis. Me vera talium Præsentia Cogitare Juvat, Dicere vetat. Quandoquidem vos et vestras excellentias intueor, quò Doctrinam Supremas virtutes amplectantem, vero nitore elucentem, quò artes Omnes sibi Mutuò amicè Respondentes Conspicio; Confestìm Admiratio mea Doctrinæ Deintegrò Excitatur:

O! istius Præstantia quod ità Homines exornat, quod illos supra Genus Humanum evehit, quo Profunditates naturæ inexplorabiles explorantur; Pennæ Sublimes, quibus supra hanc Pilulam terrestrem elevamur, et altum volatu Petimus Regiones istas immensas, quibus Orbes isti Promicantes Circulis Infinitis, Gyro Æterno, perpetuò volvuntur: Quo Campos Æthereos, istos usquequàque Coruscos Perambulamus vos Beati Scholastici qui his Rebus tali modo versamini; Nos miseri qui ab illis Desinentes; nunc voluptatibus istiusmodi nobis, et Omnibus aliis mihi videtur; vobis etiam, tum nos his exercitationibus Ducentibus,[^015-note50] tum vobis nos in istis Concomitantibus est Valedicendum.

Tibi Itaque primò Literis Eximie Cum primis [^015-note51] Reverende Domine TIMOTHEE CUTLER Gymnasiarcha Clarissime ac Rector noster Honorate, ter Honorande, supplicitèr et Infimâ Animi Demissione vocem Permittito vertere. Quemadmodum te Continuò Persentimus Hujus Gymnasii Corporis, vitæ et motionis fontem perennem, Spirituum animalium originem, quibus assiduè Præstolabamur, quibusq; indiès Animati fuimus. Nunc Atque exhilaramur: Quemadmodum Accademiam Videmus influentiis tuis Benignis ad Perfectam staturam Infantiâ sanè suâ tam subitò Reductam: Quemadmodum foti sumus a Radiis Istis Circumquàque a te Diffusis, in Medio Hujus systematis Literarii, sicut a sole in medio sui; ibidè atque Languida illa Doctrina sublustris nobis Emanans, se nil Amplius esse Reflectione tuæ Lucis vividè Circumfulgent [. . .]

Domine Vale

Tibi Deinceps quàm Literis Versate, valdè venerande Domine IOSEPHE NOYES noviporti Dei Verbi Minister Eminentissime; nec satis Gratulande; Quandoquidem ista tam Scientia Sacra, quam humana et Accademica, non animo tue solo, non sanè tam Limitibus vastis fuit Restricta; Ab illa autefonte fluenta superfluentia fluebant; Cujus Ore sæpenumero sacra Dei Oracula nobis administrata fuerunt: Quo institiones Dei salutiferas, Claras διδαχω'ν τω'ν θεοσδοϖτων αυϖτω'ν explicationes, frequenter sacra ab ista sede exaudivimus; hisce Domine Muneribus non Hisce tantum, sed omnibus innumeris nobisneque merentibus neque querentibus exhibitis. Gratitudinem nostram accipias. Secundum Desideriutuum indubitandum, pro eviterno Animarum bono, in istum finem Deus Omnipotens functione illa sacra qua te Locavit secund[. . .]

verit, et [. . .]

Proximò tibi Honorabissime et impendiò Literate Domine DANIEL BROWN hujus Accademiæ tutor Præclare, tibi (Inquam) Domine, Literis Ornato, Literis Ornamento, ac superbi Dicere sumus nostro Ornamentario, nos Gratias Imo Pectore Agimus et voce reagimus, tam quod ecquando Hanc Scholam (te Causa) egregiam, tutorius Honestasti, permansione tua Decorasti, atque Presentiâ tuâ nunc Decoras; Quam nobis te Continuò Beneficisse (non Dicam Beasse) Cautis Inspectionibus, tuitione Præstanti, Candidissimis exemplis, Multisque Benevolentiæ testimoniis Gratias habemus. tibi Oblectantes Jucunditates, Elevantes animam voluptates Omnes, Lusu Illigato immensarum Idearum fluentes, interminata Cogitationis exercitatione Orientes Peroptamus: Omnes etiam Beatitu[dines] immortales virtute quam Possides

[. . .. . . . .]perfuturas esse Precamur.

[. . . . . . . . . . . . .]Voluptatis
